The near-zero Holm and BH rates at 𝐵 = 100 and 200 are explained by the reachability floor of Equation 4: with 20 comparisons a single standout cannot be flagged below 𝐵 = 400. BH can flag below that when several items sit at the Monte Carlo floor at once, which is rare under the null, and Holm flagged nothing. From 𝐵 = 400 Holm was between 2.6 and 3.7% and BH between 2.7 and 3.7%. The decline of the WY rate between 𝐵 = 100 and 400 is the studentization effect described in the section on bootstrap p-values. With joint studentization the rate was between 3.1 and 3.5% at every 𝐵, and paired within replications the standard version exceeded the joint one by 2.0 percentage points at 𝐵 = 100 (SE 0.3) and by at most 0.1 points from 𝐵 = 1000. The excess at small 𝐵 is therefore a property of the studentization rather than of the step-down.

What remains is a level slightly below 5% that does not change between 𝐵 = 400 and 10 000 under either version, and the per-item marginal tests flagged 4.6 to 4.8% of fitting items at every 𝐵. An error that does not shrink as 𝐵 grows is not Monte Carlo error but the finite-sample departure that Ferreira (2024, Section 5) notes for bootstrap methods with estimated parameters, here conservative. It was smaller in the polytomous studies, at 4.2 and 4.7% from 𝐵 = 400.

The reachability floor also shapes detection. With a single misfitting item at 𝑛 = 150, BH detection across 𝐵 = 100 to 2000 was 0, 7, 100, 100, 100%, jumping exactly where 𝐵 crosses the floor of 400. With three misfitting items the sequence was 17, 76, 83, 82, 84%, with substantial detection already at 𝐵 = 200, because three p-values at the Monte Carlo floor lower the attainable adjusted p-value to 𝑚/(3(𝐵 + 1)). Holm detected nothing below its floor in either condition (0, 0, 100, 100, 100% and 0, 0, 71, 72, 76%), since several standouts do not lower it. The WY rule was flat across the whole range at 100, 100, 100, 100, 100% and 77, 78, 78, 77, 77% respectively, since it needs only 𝐵 ≥ 20 to reject and its power does not depend on 𝐵 beyond that. Figure 3 compares all four rules at the anchored misfit level. Against the interval at 𝑤 = .999 the WY rule detects substantially more misfit, by 8.8 to 18.3 percentage points across the non-saturated nine-item conditions at 𝐵 = 2000, at a false positive rate of 1.2% against 0.6% for the interval, pooled across arms, so it detects more at a higher cost. Part of this raw gain, however, reflects the interval's implicit error rate rather than the method. By Equation 1, 𝑤 = .999 corresponds to a familywise rate of only 0.9% for nine items, so the interval is operating far below the 𝛼 = .05 of the p-value rules.

A fair comparison holds the error rate constant. For nine items, 𝑤 = .995 implies a familywise rate of 4.4% by Equation 1, and its observed null rate at 𝐵 = 2000 was 5.3%, closely matching the p-value rules. Against this matched interval the WY rule still detected more misfit in every non-saturated condition, by 1.7 to 5.0 percentage points, with slightly fewer false positives. The remaining advantage comes from studentization and from using the joint distribution of the item statistics. More important than its size is what the matching exercise reveals: an interval width that matches a chosen 𝛼 must be solved from Equation 1for each item count, and for most item counts no convenient width exists. The WY rule targets 𝛼 directly, whatever the number of items.

Table S4 reports Holm by condition. At 𝐵 = 100 it detected 0.0% of the misfitting items, since the floor of Equation 4 is 180 iterations for nine items and 300 for fifteen. At 𝐵 = 2000 the WY rule detected between -1.7 and 1.7 percentage points more than Holm across conditions, paired within replications, with standard errors of up to 2.0 points, and the pooled false positive rates were 1.2% against 1.0%. Using the joint distribution of the item statistics therefore adds little power here, as Ferreira's (2024) account leads one to expect for statistics that are close to independent. The practical advantage of the step-down over Holm is that it needs no minimum number of iterations.

A short scale tests BH where the floor does not bind at all. At five items the floor is 100 iterations, so the procedure operates freely at every 𝐵 examined, and the comparison is no longer handicapped by reachability. At 𝑛 = 250 it detected the misfitting item in 91.2% of replications against 90.8% for the WY rule, with false positives of 6.6% against 4.2%. At 𝑛 = 600 both detected it in every replication, with false positives of 22.7% against 16.3%. Paired within replications, the detection difference was within one standard error of zero at both sample sizes while the false positive difference exceeded four. The preference for the WY rule is therefore not an artifact of testing BH only where its floor binds.

BH and WY performed similarly with a single misfitting item, while with several misfitting items BH detected somewhat more, as expected since the false discovery rate is a more permissive criterion when true effects are present. The price appears on the other side of the ledger, with pooled false positives of 2.3% against 1.2% at 𝐵 = 2000, roughly a doubling. The marginal p-value, finally, is close to its nominal level per item but unusable as a screening rule, as its familywise rate of around 60% shows. Once items misfit its false positive rate on the fitting items rises, which the section on error rates under misfit returns to. Taken together, these results support the WY p-value as the default among the decision rules examined, with Holm a close alternative once 𝐵 clears its floor, the marginal p-value reserved for a single pre-specified item, and BH for longer instruments where a false discovery criterion is acceptable and 𝐵 clears the reachability floor. The interval retains descriptive value, which the Discussion returns to.

6.2 Conditional outfit under the same rules

Every result so far concerns conditional infit. The same bootstrap produces conditional outfit at no additional cost, and the decision rules are indifferent to which statistic they are given, so it is worth asking whether anything is gained by reading both. Outfit is also widely reported in its unconditional form, for example by RUMM2030, a widely used Rasch analysis software package. The comparison here concerns conditional outfit only, and its results need not carry over to the unconditional statistic, whose behavior under the model differs (Müller, 2020). Johansson (2025) compared conditional infit and outfit for dichotomous items under the interval rule and found outfit worse throughout. The sixth study revisits that comparison under the corrected p-value and extends it to polytomous items. The two item sets were placed at correlations that inject comparable infit, 1.28 for the dichotomous set and 1.27 for the phq9 set, both inside the range the real phq9 items reach. A single misfitting item was placed either near the person mean or off target, the WY rule was applied at 𝐵 = 1000 and 𝛼 = .05, and both statistics were read from the same bootstrap within each replication.

Under the null conditions the FWER was 2.4 to 4.8% for outfit against 3.6 to 6.4% for infit, across the four combinations of item set and sample size, with a Monte Carlo standard error of 1.4 points. The marginal rates before the step-down were 3.8 to 4.9% and 4.4 to 5.3%. Neither the statistic nor the correction is miscalibrated, so the studentized maximum needs no modification when the statistic it is given changes.

Detection is a different matter. Infit detected the misfitting item more often than outfit in every one of the eight conditions, and Table 4 gives the margins. The gap is not small, ranging from 4 to 41 percentage points. Outfit is the unweighted mean of squared standardized residuals and is conventionally expected to earn its place on items far from the person mean, and the injected outfit is indeed larger there. Detection nevertheless moves the other way. Averaged over item sets and sample sizes the infit advantage was 16 points for the on-target item and 33 points for the off-target one. The null conditions explain why. Outfit is the more variable statistic under a true model, by a factor of 2.9 in the dichotomous set and 1.4 in the phq9 set, and within the dichotomous set that factor is 2.2 for the five most central items and 3.6 for the five most extreme. The variance grows faster than the signal exactly where outfit was expected to help.

Because both statistics come from the same bootstrap, the comparison can be made replication by replication rather than only on the margins. Table 4 reports the discordant counts. Replications in which outfit flagged the misfitting item and infit did not never exceeded 14 of 250, against up to 108 in the other direction, and every condition favours infit at 𝑝 < .01 by an exact test on the discordant pairs. In these conditions outfit added little to what infit detected.

This has a direct consequence for practice, because both statistics often appear side by side in the standard output. Flagging an item when either statistic is significant tests 2𝑘 hypotheses while each correction covers 𝑘, and the null FWER of that combined rule was 6.4 to 8.8%, against a nominal 5% and the 2.4 to 6.4% the two statistics reach on their own. The inflation is below the doubling that independent statistics would produce, since the two are computed on the same responses and are positively dependent, but it is real and adds almost no detection. For conditional statistics at these settings, the results favor deciding on infit and treating outfit as descriptive. Note. Westfall-Young (WY) rule at 𝐵 = 1000 and 𝛼 = .05, 250 replications per condition. Det is the percentage of replications in which the misfitting item was flagged. Only infit and Only outfit count the replications in which one statistic flagged it and the other did not. The p-value is an exact binomial test on those discordant counts. Design and false positive rates are in Table S6.

6.3 Random responding under the same rules

Every result so far induces misfit through multidimensionality, so two of them could in principle be properties of that mechanism rather than of misfit in general. The first is the false positives that appear on fitting items once misfitting items are present. The second is the masking that weakens detection when two items misfit together. The mechanism check separates the two. It holds the item set, the conditions and the decision rules fixed, and replaces multidimensionality with random responding at matched infit. The false positives are not specific to multidimensionality. With two misfitting items at 𝑛 = 1000 and 𝐵 = 2000, random responding produced false positives on the seven fitting items of 11.3% under the interval rule at 𝑤 = .999 and 22.5% under the WY rule, against 1.7% and 4.8% under multidimensionality. Nearly all false flags pointed in the overfit direction under the interval rule for both mechanisms, at 100% and 97% of flags. Direction is read from the band, so the p-value rules cannot be scored the same way. What puts a fitting item outside its expected range is contamination of the total score that it is conditioned on, rather than the particular way that contamination arises.

The error rate results are therefore not an artifact of the mechanism, and the multidimensional condition is the more forgiving of the two.

Masking is mechanism-specific, and should be read as such. Two independently noisy items do not mask each other, and detection of the two target items at 𝑛 = 250 was 55.4% under random responding against 31.7% under multidimensionality. Masking requires the misfitting items to share a source. That is the case worth planning for, since a secondary dimension rarely involves a single item, but it is not a property of misfit in general.

6.4 Error rates when some items misfit

The error rates reported so far were measured under the null. With misfitting items present, strong FWER control concerns the probability of flagging at least one fitting item, and BH targets the false discovery rate, the expected proportion of flagged items that in fact fit. Table S5 reports both for every condition with misfitting items, at 𝐵 = 2000.

At sample sizes of 250 or below the rates were close to nominal. At the anchored misfit level in the polytomous sets, the WY rate over the fitting items was between 0.8 and 8.3%, and the false discovery rate of BH between 1.7 and 7.0%. At 𝑛 = 1000 every procedure exceeded its nominal level. For the nine phq9 items, the WY rate over fitting items was 25.0% with one misfitting item and 30.0% with two, the Holm rate 21.7% and 26.7%, and the false discovery rate of BH 18.3% and 19.5%. The rates grew with misfit severity, reaching 72.5% for WY at 𝜌 = .75, and were higher under random responding, at 82.5% with two misfitting items at 𝑛 = 1000.

The source of the excess lies in the per-item tests rather than in the corrections. At 𝑛 = 1000 the uncorrected marginal p-value flagged 13.6% of the fitting phq9 items with one misfitting item and 19.8% with two, where a valid per-item test would flag about 5%. Each fitting item's statistic is conditioned on a total score that includes the misfitting items, so its distribution changes when they misfit, while the bootstrap reference is generated from a model in which all items fit. This is the failure of subset pivotality described earlier, and Ferreira (2024, p. 2) notes that a p-value can have the wrong distribution because the model is incorrect rather than because its null hypothesis is false. It also explains why Holm, which needs no pivotality condition but relies on valid marginal p-values, failed in the same conditions. The procedures studied here therefore provide weak FWER control, and no correction applied to these per-item tests can provide strong control once misfit contaminates the conditioning score. The contamination grows with sample size and misfit severity, and removing the most misfitting item before judging the rest reduces it.

The false discovery rate of BH stayed close to nominal where the per-item tests were close to valid and exceeded it where they were not, and the WY rule had a false discovery rate at or below it in every condition. The preference for a familywise criterion rests on the use of the analysis. A scale of 5 to 20 items is judged item by item, each flag leads to an item being considered for removal, and a single false flag changes the instrument. The false discovery rate is most useful when many hypotheses are tested and many discoveries are expected (Ferreira, 2024), which is rarely the case in item fit analysis.

7 Decision stability

The previous sections report error rates averaged over many simulated datasets, but an analyst has one dataset, and two analysts who run the same analysis on it with different random seeds can reach different conclusions. To quantify how often, ten datasets per condition were held fixed and each was analyzed with 25 bootstrap seeds. The observed infit values are then fixed, and all variation between seeds is simulation noise.

For each item and dataset, the flag rate 𝑝 is the proportion of the 25 seeds in which the item was flagged, and an item is counted as unstable when 0 < 𝑝 < 1. Twenty-five seeds resolve 𝑝 in steps of 0.04, so the unstable counts below are lower bounds. Two analyses disagree when their sets of flagged items differ, and the probability of this is measured directly as the proportion of the 300 pairs of seeds that disagree, where 𝑐 𝑗 is the number of seeds that produced the 𝑗-th distinct set of flagged items and 𝑆 = 25 is the number of seeds.

𝐷 is computed within each dataset, averaged over the ten datasets of a condition, and needs no assumption about how the items depend on each other. For a single item the corresponding probability is 2𝑝(1 -𝑝), the chance that one of two seeds flags the item and the other does not.

Under the null conditions, flagging with the WY p-value is essentially reproducible. Pooled across both item sets and sample sizes, 1.2% of items had a seed-dependent flag at 𝐵 = 100, 0.2% at 𝐵 = 400, and none at 𝐵 = 2000. At the anchored misfit level (𝜌 = .85), pooled the same way, 3.3% of items were unstable at 𝐵 = 400 and 1.0% at 𝐵 = 2000 (Monte Carlo standard error 0.7 percentage points), against 10.6% and 6.0% for the interval rule at 𝑤 = .999. Pooled at the anchored level, the probability that two seeds using the WY rule disagree about at least one item was 23% at 𝐵 = 100, 10% at 𝐵 = 400, 6% at 𝐵 = 1000, and 4% at 𝐵 = 2000. 𝐵 = 400 is thus where the error rates are trustworthy, but reproducibility keeps improving well past it.

Treating the items as flipping independently, with each 2𝑝(1 -𝑝) multiplied by 𝑆/(𝑆 -1) to remove the bias of estimating 𝑝 from 25 seeds, gives nearly the same pooled values, within 0.2 percentage points. The average hides where independence fails. For the WY rule the independence product overstated the measured disagreement by more than 5 percentage points in 44 of 480 combinations of dataset and number of iterations, by up to 37 points, and never understated it by as much. In those datasets one seed's maximum statistic moves several decisions together, the coupling that the step-down introduces. For the interval rule, whose items are judged separately, the two agreed within 8 points in every dataset. S8.

Figure 4 shows that instability is not monotone in misfit severity or sample size. At 𝐵 = 2000 the strongest misfit (𝜌 = .15) left on average 1.1 items per dataset with a seed-dependent flag at 𝑛 = 250 but 0.0 at 𝑛 = 1000, where items were flagged or cleared decisively. Instability arises where the evidence about an item approaches the flagging threshold without clearly passing it, so it is driven by the number of such boundary items rather than by misfit severity or sample size as such. Across the misfit conditions, the probability that two seeds disagreed at 𝐵 = 2000 ranged from 0 to 23%. With ten datasets per condition, these results illustrate the dependence rather than giving rates that carry over to other data.

How much instability an analysis shows is thus set by the data. If an item's p-value with unlimited iterations is 𝜋, its Monte Carlo estimate from 𝐵 iterations has a standard error of about √𝜋(1 -𝜋)/𝐵, so the iterations needed for a reproducible decision grow with the inverse square of the distance between 𝜋 and 𝛼, and for items close to the threshold no affordable number of iterations removes the instability. Seed sensitivity is therefore a diagnostic. Items whose flags change with the seed are borderline cases that need more data or substantive judgment rather than a luckier seed. The instability of the interval rule at 𝑤 = .999 declined only slowly with 𝐵, consistent with its extreme quantiles remaining poorly estimated throughout the practical range.

8 Discussion

This paper asked how the interval width and the number of bootstrap iterations should be chosen for simulation-based cutoffs, and whether the interval is the best way to turn a bootstrap into a decision about item fit. The answers are connected. The width is the FWER parameter of the interval rule and is described well by Equation 1. The number of iterations required is set by the width, and not by the sample size that the earlier guidance conditioned on. The same bootstrap also supports a p-value rule that targets a chosen error rate directly, detects more misfit at a matched error rate, and needs far fewer iterations.

Detection at the anchored misfit level was modest. With the nine phq9-derived items and a single misfitting item at 𝜌 = .85, the WY rule detected the misfit in 62.5% of replications at 𝑛 = 250 and 50% at 𝑛 = 150, reaching 100% only at 𝑛 = 1000, and the interval rule detected less. Two misfitting items were harder to detect than one. When q8 misfit alongside q5, detection of q5 at 𝑛 = 250 fell from 62.5% to 47.5%, because items that share a secondary dimension are evaluated against person estimates contaminated by that dimension. At 𝑛 = 250 or below, an item fit analysis that flags nothing is therefore weak evidence that nothing is wrong, and other methods of evaluating fit should be used alongside it.

One conclusion of Johansson (2025) is confirmed and extended. That paper found conditional outfit worse than conditional infit for dichotomous items under the interval rule. Under the WY rule at 𝐵 = 1000, conditional outfit was calibrated but detected less misfit than infit in every condition studied, for polytomous items as well as dichotomous ones. Its weakness is one of power, and it follows from its larger variability under a true model, which grows with distance from the person mean, so the off-target items that outfit is conventionally recommended for are the ones it handles worst.

8.1 Recommendations for practice

Five recommendations follow from the results. 1. Decide with the corrected p-value. Among the decision rules examined, the WY step-down at 𝛼 = .05 performed best, and it is the recommended decision rule for conditional item fit. Its FWER under the null was at or slightly below the nominal level from 𝐵 = 400 onward. Holm gave nearly the same results once 𝐵 cleared its floor of 𝑘/𝛼 iterations, but the step-down needs no such floor. It also detected more misfit than the interval rule at a matched error rate, and it was the more stable of the two under reseeding. Against BH it holds up even where that procedure is unconstrained: on a five-item scale, whose reachability floor of 100 iterations binds at no 𝐵 examined here, the two detected the misfitting item equally often while BH flagged roughly half again as many fitting ones. The control holds when all items fit, and once items misfit the false flags on fitting items grow with sample size for every rule, which the fifth recommendation addresses. 2. Use at least 400 iterations, and more when computation allows. Below 400 the WY rule was mildly liberal under the null, at 5.6% at 𝐵 = 100, and decisions were noticeably seeddependent. The probability that two analysts using different seeds disagree about at least one item kept falling past that point, from 10% at 𝐵 = 400 to 4% at 𝐵 = 2000, so at least 1000 to 2000 iterations are worth the cost for a final analysis. Neither the sample size nor the number of items sets this requirement. The step-down can reject from 𝐵 = 1/𝛼 = 20 at any scale length, its null rate from 𝐵 = 400 was between 3.4 and 5.0% across the 5, 9, 15 and 20-item sets with no trend in the number of items, and the floor of 400 is set by calibration and reproducibility. Holm and BH, in contrast, cannot flag even a single standout item below 𝑘/𝛼 iterations, a floor that grows with the number of items. 3. Report the interval as a description and set its width to .95. At w = .95, 1000 iterations bring the interval within 1% of the width it reaches with 10 000, so the band shown to readers covers close to the 95% it claims. At w = .999 the same 1% takes 5000 iterations, far more than is usually run, so the stated width is not the width the reader is actually seeing. A band showing where a fitting item's infit is expected to fall is worth reporting, but only when its stated width is real. 4. Report the number of iterations and the random seed. Both are part of the analysis.

Without them a reader cannot tell whether a borderline item would have been flagged in a rerun. The seed will however only be useful if the data are also shared. 5. Remove items one at a time. This is the workflow recommended by Johansson (2025), and its sequential error rates were not evaluated here, since every reported rate refers to a single analysis. It is retained because misfitting items contaminate the total score that the other items are judged against, which is the source of the false flags on fitting items when some items misfit, and removing the most misfitting item first reduces that contamination before the remaining items are judged. Repeated testing across removal rounds adds multiplicity that the corrections studied here do not address, as the Limitations discuss. Versions of easyRasch2 before 1.2.0 used 𝑤 = .999 and 𝐵 = 250 by default, a setting at which the interval equals the range of the simulated values. From version 1.2.0 the package flags items on the WY p-value, shows the interval at 𝑤 = .95 and runs 400 iterations by default, and the upcoming version 1.4.1 studentizes the observed and simulated statistics jointly. These recommendations are not confined to users of R. The same analyses are available through easyRasch2jmv (Johansson, 2026b), a module for the jamovi statistical platform (The jamovi project, 2026) that delegates every computation to easyRasch2, so that results are numerically identical given the same seeds and iteration counts. Simulation-based cutoffs can be more demanding to use than a rule of thumb, since they require a decision about the number of iterations and about which decision rule to apply, and the recommendations given here are meant to make those decisions simpler.

9 Limitations

Misfit was induced by two mechanisms only, multidimensionality throughout and random responding in the mechanism check. Other mechanisms, such as local dependence and genuine variation in item discrimination, were not examined.

Each replication is a single pass through the data. No study simulates the iterative workflow in which a flagged item is removed and the remaining items reanalyzed, which is how the procedures are used in practice and the response to their lack of strong control. Its error rates, including the multiplicity added by repeated testing across removal rounds, are the most important open question.

The scope is narrow in further respects. The item sets had 5, 9, 15, and 20 items, so Equation 1 and the iteration requirements are verified in that range and not for longer instruments. The comparison of conditional infit and outfit was made at one decision rule and iteration count, the WY p-value with 𝐵 = 1000, and does not necessarily extend to the unconditional outfit that some software reports. Of the FWER procedures only the WY step-down and Holm were compared, and resampling-based false discovery rate procedures (Yekutieli & Benjamini, 1999) were not included. Benjamini-Hochberg is guaranteed to control the false discovery rate under positive regression dependency (Benjamini & Yekutieli, 2001), but whether that extends to two-sided tests on a residual-based statistic such as conditional infit is open, so its rates here rest on the simulations alone. Joint studentization was evaluated in the dichotomous null conditions only. The two versions differ only in whether the observed value contributes to the moments, a share of 1/(𝐵 + 1), and in those conditions the difference in familywise error was 0.5 percentage points at 𝐵 = 400 and at most 0.1 from 𝐵 = 1000. A similarly small difference is expected in the polytomous sets and under misfit at the recommended numbers of iterations, but this was not verified. Exact validity at a given sample size, by maximizing the Monte Carlo p-value over the nuisance parameters (Dufour, 2006) or, for dichotomous items, by conditioning on the observed margins (Verhelst, 2008), was not examined. Every study used 𝛼 = .05. The reachability floors and the correspondence between width and familywise error hold at any 𝛼, but the reported rates and the 400-iteration floor were established at .05, and a smaller 𝛼 would likely require more iterations. The anchored misfit levels were calibrated against a single instrument with an unusual configuration, two items far above the person mean, and the stylised 15-item set is the only regular configuration it is compared with. Matching on mean infit, finally, does not make different misfit mechanisms equivalent, as the mechanism check shows.

A practical limit appears at the short end. Five polytomous items with four response categories imply fifteen threshold parameters, and at 𝑛 = 250 2 of the 3748 bootstraps run in that study produced no usable p-value at all, their simulated statistics having too little variance to studentize by. This did not occur at 𝑛 = 600 or with 9 items or more, but a five-item scale at a small sample is where the procedure starts to strain.

10 Use of large language models

Large language models were used in preparing this work. The author designed the studies, decided which conditions to run and which results to report, verified the code and the reported values, and is responsible for all content including any errors.

A large language model was used in an agentic coding environment to write and revise the simulation code, to run the studies, and to produce the tables and figures from the cached results. It was also used to draft and revise manuscript text against an outline and instructions specified by the author, and to check the drafted text against the cached results.

Note. Percentages at 𝐵 = 2000. FWER is the percentage of replications in which at least one fitting item was flagged, and FDR the mean proportion of flagged items that were fitting items, counted as 0 when no item was flagged. 𝑘 is the number of misfitting items. The polytomous sets are at the anchored misfit level (𝜌 = .85, 120 replications), the dichotomous set at 𝜌 = .15 (250 replications), and random responding at matched infit (120 replications). Holm is not available for the randomresponding conditions, whose marginal p-values were not stored. WY is the Westfall-Young stepdown and BH the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure.

Infit and outfit by condition

Table S6 gives the design of the sixth study together with the misfit it injects on both scales and the false positive rates it produces. The injected infit is comparable across the two item sets by construction, which is what makes the detection comparison in Table 4 interpretable. The injected outfit is not comparable, being substantially larger in the dichotomous set, and larger for the off-target item than the on-target one within each set. Detection nonetheless runs the other way, which is the point made in the section on conditional outfit.

Monte Carlo error of the reported estimates

The replication counts differ between studies because the studies estimate different quantities with different precision requirements. Table S7 reports the Monte Carlo standard error of the key estimate from each study, computed from the cached simulation results. Proportions over independent replications use the binomial standard error. The interval convergence medians and the stability percentages use a cluster bootstrap that resamples replications or datasets, which respects the dependence of items within a dataset. The rule comparisons in the PHQ-9-anchored misfit study are paired within replications, since all decision rules are computed from the same bootstrap, and their standard errors are correspondingly small. Two consequences for interpretation follow. First, every directional claim in the paper rests on a contrast that is at least three times its standard error, or on a pattern replicated across several conditions. Second, the per-condition stability percentages are the least precise estimates in the paper, which is why the Decision stability section pools them across item sets and sample sizes and the per-condition values in Table S8 are descriptive.

Note. Convergence: cluster bootstrap over replications, worst case shown (𝑤 = .999, 𝐵 = 2000, 𝑛 = 250). Error rates: binomial SE at the stated proportion with 500 replications, the null replications per sample size. PHQ-9-anchored misfit: paired per-replication SE of the detection gain of the FWER p-value over the interval rule, range over the ten conditions at the anchored misfit level. Stability: dataset-cluster bootstrap of the pooled and per-condition unstable-item percentages at the anchored misfit level. Disagreement: the same bootstrap applied to the probability that two seeds disagree about at least one item (Equation 5), range over the four iteration counts. That marginal standard error overstates the uncertainty of the comparison between iteration counts, which is paired within dataset: the decline from B = 400 to B = 2000 is 5.9 points with a standard error of 1.6. Mechanism: SE of the false positive contrast between misfit mechanisms. Infit versus outfit: paired per-replication SE of the detection gap between the two statistics, range over the eight conditions. Short scale: paired per-replication SE of the false positive gap between Benjamini-Hochberg and Westfall-Young at five items, range over the two sample sizes. Calibration: SE of the mean infit at the calibrated level.

Reproducibility

Everything needed to reproduce this paper is in the repository at https://github.com/pgmj/rasch_fwer, which contains the manuscript source, the simulation code, and the cached simulation results. The manuscript is written as a Quarto document (Allaire et al., 2026). Every number reported in the body, including those in the figures and tables, is computed at render time from the cached results rather than transcribed, so the reported values cannot drift from the data behind them.

The abstract is the one exception: Quarto evaluates the document metadata before the code that loads the caches, so the few figures quoted there are typed and were checked against the body by hand. Rendering the manuscript reproduces the whole paper in seconds without rerunning any simulation. This is also relevant to assessing the use of large language models described in the main text. No reported number depends on a model or a person correctly copying it, and any reported quantity can be recomputed independently of the process that produced it.

The studies are in simulations/, each writing its results to an .rds file beside it, apart from the two dichotomous studies, which were rerun for the revision with fresh data in every replication and are in rev1/ (fresh_rerun.R). The original master-dataset versions of those two studies remain in simulations/ for comparison. Three further scripts hold the calibration scans that fix the misfit levels, two of which the manuscript reads directly for Table 2 and for the five-item calibration in the Method. Shared code, including the data generators and the plot theme, is in R/helpers.R. The seven studies comprise 18 180 design cells and took about 67 hours to run on ten M4 cores. Each study uses a resumable runner. Design cells are keyed on the columns of the design grid, results are checkpointed to disk after every chunk, and cells already present in the cache are skipped, so raising a replication count and rerunning computes only the new cells. Seeds are deterministic functions of the design cell rather than of the execution order, which means any single cell can be reproduced on its own without running the study that contains it.

All results were produced with R 4.6.1 (R Core Team, 2026) and easyRasch2 (Johansson, 2026a), under versions 1.1.0 and 1.1.1 and, for the two dichotomous studies, 1.4.0. As a check, a cell of the original dichotomous study rebuilt under 1.4.0 reproduced its p-values and interval flags to the last digit. The package computes conditional item fit through iarm 0.4.3 (Mueller & Santiago, 2022) and estimates item parameters by conditional maximum likelihood with psychotools 0.7.6 (Zeileis et al., 2026). The multidimensional dichotomous data were generated with eRm 1.0.10 (Mair & Hatzinger, 2007), the polytomous data with the generators in easyRasch2, and the highest density intervals computed with ggdist 3.3.3 (Kay, 2025). The phq9 data used to calibrate misfit severity are included in easyRasch2.

Figure 1.

Figure 1. Convergence of the cutoff interval Note. Median cutoff interval width at each number of iterations, relative to the width at 10 000 iterations of the same bootstrap, for four sample sizes and five interval widths. Each line is one combination of width and sample size, with the width shown by line type and point shape. Lines of the same width overlap, showing that convergence does not depend on sample size. Values below 1 indicate an interval narrower than its nominal width.Second, the number of iterations required grows sharply as the interval widens. Taking 99% of that reference width as a criterion, 1000 iterations suffice for 𝑤 = .95, 2000 for 𝑤 = .99, and 5000 for 𝑤 = .999. The reference is empirical rather than asymptotic: at 𝑤 = .999 it has only five draws

Figure 2.

Figure 2. Familywise false positive rate against the prediction of Equation 1

Figure 3.

Figure 3. Detection and false positives for the four decision rules Note. Detection rate (misfitting items) and false positive rate (fitting items) at the anchored misfit level, by number of iterations, item set, and sample size. Rates are averaged over the misfittingitem conditions. Note the different y-axis scales for the two rows.Figure3compares all four rules at the anchored misfit level. Against the interval at 𝑤 = .999 the WY rule detects substantially more misfit, by 8.8 to 18.3 percentage points across the non-saturated nine-item conditions at 𝐵 = 2000, at a false positive rate of 1.2% against 0.6% for the interval, pooled across arms, so it detects more at a higher cost. Part of this raw gain, however, reflects the interval's implicit error rate rather than the method. By Equation 1, 𝑤 = .999 corresponds to a familywise rate of only 0.9% for nine items, so the interval is operating far below the 𝛼 = .05 of the p-value rules.A fair comparison holds the error rate constant. For nine items, 𝑤 = .995 implies a familywise rate of 4.4% by Equation 1, and its observed null rate at 𝐵 = 2000 was 5.3%, closely matching the p-value rules. Against this matched interval the WY rule still detected more misfit in every non-saturated condition, by 1.7 to 5.0 percentage points, with slightly fewer false positives. The remaining advantage comes from studentization and from using the joint distribution of the item statistics. More important than its size is what the matching exercise reveals: an interval width that matches a chosen 𝛼 must be solved from Equation1for each item count, and for most item counts no convenient width exists. The WY rule targets 𝛼 directly, whatever the number of items.TableS4reports Holm by condition. At 𝐵 = 100 it detected 0.0% of the misfitting items, since the floor of Equation 4 is 180 iterations for nine items and 300 for fifteen. At 𝐵 = 2000 the WY rule detected between -1.7 and 1.7 percentage points more than Holm across conditions, paired within replications, with standard errors of up to 2.0 points, and the pooled false positive rates were 1.2% against 1.0%. Using the joint distribution of the item statistics therefore adds little power here, asFerreira's (2024) account leads one to expect for statistics that are close to independent. The practical advantage of the step-down over Holm is that it needs no minimum number of iterations.A short scale tests BH where the floor does not bind at all. At five items the floor is 100 iterations, so the procedure operates freely at every 𝐵 examined, and the comparison is no longer handicapped by reachability. At 𝑛 = 250 it detected the misfitting item in 91.2% of replications

Figure 4.

Figure 4. Seed dependence of flagging decisions Note. Percentage of items whose flagging decision depends on the bootstrap seed (25 seeds per dataset, 10 datasets per condition), by number of iterations, misfit severity, decision rule, item set, and sample size. Lower values of the inter-dimensional correlation mean stronger misfit. Percondition values carry Monte Carlo standard errors of up to about 3 percentage points and are tabulated in TableS8. Figure4shows that instability is not monotone in misfit severity or sample size. At 𝐵 = 2000 the strongest misfit (𝜌 = .15) left on average 1.1 items per dataset with a seed-dependent flag at 𝑛 = 250 but 0.0 at 𝑛 = 1000, where items were flagged or cleared decisively. Instability arises where the evidence about an item approaches the flagging threshold without clearly passing it, so it is driven by the number of such boundary items rather than by misfit severity or sample size as such. Across the misfit conditions, the probability that two seeds disagreed at 𝐵 = 2000 ranged from 0 to 23%. With ten datasets per condition, these results illustrate the dependence rather than giving rates that carry over to other data.How much instability an analysis shows is thus set by the data. If an item's p-value with unlimited iterations is 𝜋, its Monte Carlo estimate from 𝐵 iterations has a standard error of about √𝜋(1 -𝜋)/𝐵, so the iterations needed for a reproducible decision grow with the inverse square
